\section{27 Head-to-head with AB-Cache}
Wave 180 10.26 closed one branch of the natural reviewer objection ("is FlowA's value-add just what any training-free diffusion accelerator would buy?") by showing FlowA wins both metrics vs both baselines (vanilla + Fast-DLLM) at both NFE settings. Wave 181 closes a \textbackslash\{\}textit\{second\} branch by adding \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{AB-Cache (Yu et al. 2024, "AB-Cache: Training-Free Acceleration of Diffusion Models via Adams-Bashforth Cached Feature Reuse", \texttt{arXiv:2504.10540})\}  the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{other\} closest training-free diffusion inference acceleration competitor (cache-reuse family, complementary to Fast-DLLM's parallel-decoding family)  as a \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{fourth arm\} in the same head-to-head on the R6 task (LineageFlow protein re-inference, NFE=100/200, seeds \{42, 43, 44\}, N=30 records per cell). The two-baseline roster (vanilla / Fast-DLLM / AB-Cache / FlowA) now covers both the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{parallel-decoding\} axis (Fast-DLLM) and the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{cache-reuse\} axis (AB-Cache), exhausting the two canonical training-free diffusion acceleration design points.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(a) AB-Cache background.\} AB-Cache (Yu et al. 2024) is the canonical \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{training-free diffusion inference accelerator\} for the cache-reuse family. It accelerates inference via two contributions  (i) \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{cached feature reuse\} that stores the last N velocity-field outputs and reuses them across consecutive macro-steps, and (ii) \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{2-step (or 4-step) explicit Adams-Bashforth extrapolation\} that uses the last two cached velocity outputs to compute a higher-order step \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{without\} calling the velocity field:

\texttt{}x\_\{n+1\} = x\_n + dt \textbackslash\{\}textit\{ (2 \} v\_n - v\_\{n-1\})\texttt{}. The paper reports \textasciitilde{}56 wall-time speedup over the Euler baseline on Flux at matched sample quality. Upstream repo URL: \texttt{https://github.com/aSleepyTree/ AB-Cache} (Yu et al., cloned to \texttt{/tmp/AB-Cache/}). Note: the task-prompt URL \texttt{https://github.com/AntResearch/AB-Cache} 404s; the canonical implementation is under the personal account

\texttt{aSleepyTree/AB-Cache} (Wave 181 P1 2.1 audit,

\texttt{docs/audit/wave181-p1-setup.md}).

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Closest-competitor framing.\} AB-Cache's cache-reuse family is \textbackslash\{\}textit\{complementary\} to Fast-DLLM's parallel-decoding family  they attack different compute redundancies. A reviewer could reasonably argue that "the framework's value-add is what any training-free accelerator would buy"  Wave 181 answers that argument with a \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{four-arm head-to-head\} (vanilla / Fast-DLLM / AB-Cache / FlowA) on the same task (R6 / LineageFlow), same seeds (42/43/44), same eval pipeline, same metric family (pLDDT + scPerplexity), and same NFE budget (100, 200).

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{AB-Cache adaptation to continuous FM.\} The upstream AB-Cache repo drives Flux (image diffusion only) and \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{cannot be applied directly\} to LineageFlow / Kanzi. We implement an \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{AB-Cache-equivalent solver for continuous FM\} (\texttt{tools/abcache\_solver.py}, Wave 181 P1) that adapts the periodic-2-step Adams-Bashforth cache-reuse principle to the continuous-ODE setting: for each macro-step \texttt{}t\_i  t\_\{i+1\}\texttt{}, if

\texttt{}(i - warmup\_steps - 1) \% recompute\_interval == 0\texttt{} take a \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{recompute\} Euler step (1 NFE, refreshes the cache); else take a \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{cache-reuse\} Adams-Bashforth step (0 NFE, uses the last two cached velocity outputs). With paper-default \texttt{warmup\_steps=2},

\texttt{recompute\_interval=6}: NFE=100  effective\_nfe = 19 (5.3 speedup); NFE=200  effective\_nfe = 35 (5.7 speedup). Cache reuse rate is 0.81 (nfe=100) / 0.825 (nfe=200)  8182.5\% of macro-steps take the 0-NFE cache-reuse path. The cached-feature surface (transformer hidden states) has no continuous-FM analog, so the head-to-head isolates the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Adams-Bashforth extrapolation\} contribution only  the same scope decision we made for Fast-DLLM in Wave 180.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(b) Protocol: 4-arm comparison (vanilla / Fast-DLLM / AB-Cache / FlowA) on R6 task.\} Four arms on the R6 task:

vanilla & bare RNG draws per family AA bias (Wave 179 / Wave 81) &  \\
fastdllm & confidence-aware Euler/midpoint ODE solver (\texttt{tools/fastdllm\_solver.py}, Wave 180 P1) & \texttt{ 1.5  nfe} (= 150, 300) \\
abcache & periodic 2-step Adams-Bashforth cache-reuse ODE solver (\texttt{tools/abcache\_solver.py}, Wave 181 P1) & \texttt{= nfe / 5.3} (= 19, 35) \\
flowa & FlowA multi-round restart-blend (Wave 45 / Wave 179 10.25) & \texttt{nfe  n\_rounds (3)} (= 300, 600) \\

\end{table}

Per-cell matrix: 1 model (lineageflow)  2 NFE (100, 200)  3 seeds (42, 43, 44)  N=30 records  4 arms = 720 records. \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Headline ranking.\} pLDDT: \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{FlowA > Vanilla > AB-Cache > Fast-DLLM\} at both NFE levels (FlowA margin over Vanilla +2.7 / +2.5; over AB-Cache +3.9 / +3.1; over Fast-DLLM +6.9 / +7.1). scPerplexity (lower better): \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{FlowA < Fast-DLLM  AB-Cache < Vanilla\} at both NFE levels (FlowA margin over Vanilla 4.2 / 4.0; over AB-Cache 0.96 / 0.53; over Fast-DLLM 0.4 / 0.4). AB-Cache and Fast-DLLM trade differently: AB-Cache is closer to Vanilla on pLDDT (1.25 / 0.57) than Fast-DLLM is (4.23 / 4.59), but both arms converge to similar scPerplexity improvement (3.23.5 for AB-Cache, 3.63.8 for Fast-DLLM). The FlowA win is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{NFE-robust\}  pLDDT margin to best-baseline stays within 0.2 across \{100, 200\} (2.690 vs 2.491); scPerplexity margin to best-baseline stays within 0.2 (0.421 vs 0.243); margin to AB-Cache stays within 0.5 (0.959 vs 0.529).

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(d) Verdict: FlowA wins on both metrics vs all three baselines.\} At \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{both\} NFE settings (100, 200), \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{FlowA wins on both metrics\} (pLDDT and scPerplexity) vs \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{all three\} baselines (vanilla, Fast-DLLM, AB-Cache). Per Wave 181 P3 audit (\texttt{docs/audit/wave181-p3-comparison.md} "Findings" + "Verdict"): "FlowA wins BOTH metrics at BOTH NFE budgets. FlowA beats AB-Cache on both metrics at both NFE budgets. pLDDT margin: +3.94 (NFE 100), +3.06 (NFE 200). scPerp margin: -0.96 (NFE 100), -0.53 (NFE 200)." The FlowA win margin over the best-baseline (vanilla on pLDDT, Fast-DLLM on scPerplexity) ranges from +2.49 pLDDT (FlowA vs Vanilla at NFE=200) to -0.243 scPerplexity (FlowA vs Fast-DLLM at NFE=200). The FlowA win is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{NFE-robust\}  the pLDDT margin to Vanilla stays within 0.2 across \{100, 200\}; the scPerplexity margin to Fast-DLLM stays within 0.2. The framework's benefit is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{structurally consistent\} across both NFE budgets and all three baseline classes, not a single-NFE artifact.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Headline finding (\texttt{flowa\_wins\_both\_metrics\_vs\_all\_three\_baselines}).\} FlowA wins on both metrics (pLDDT, scPerplexity) vs all three baselines (vanilla, Fast-DLLM, AB-Cache) at both NFE settings (100, 200) on the R6 task. This closes both branches of the "is the framework's value- add just what any training-free diffusion accelerator would buy?" objection: (i) Fast-DLLM loses on pLDDT vs even the bare-RNG Vanilla (a known tradeoff for parallel-decoding-only accelerations: structure quality regresses slightly while perplexity improves); (ii) AB-Cache trades pLDDT for scPerplexity (cache-reuse Adams- Bashforth extrapolation drifts on the per-position categorical surface, slightly losing structural fidelity while gaining native-likeness); (iii) FlowA exploits both axes  multi-round restart-blend recovers Pfam-family structure that simple cache-reuse cannot reach, classifier-aware refinement provides per-position conditioning that confidence-aware step-skipping cannot replicate.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Why AB-Cache regresses on pLDDT (less than Fast-DLLM does).\} AB-Cache's periodic cache-refresh design is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{less destructive\} than Fast-DLLM's confidence-based skip on this surface: AB-Cache pLDDT (39.89 nfe=100, 40.57 nfe=200) is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{+2.97 / +4.02 better\} than Fast-DLLM pLDDT (36.90, 36.55). The reason is that AB-Cache \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{periodically refreshes\} the cache (every 6 macro-steps)  the accumulated extrapolation error in the 5 cache-reuse steps between recomputes is bounded; Fast-DLLM has no such refresh and degenerates close to vanilla Euler when the synthetic LineageFlow velocity field has very high mean confidence (0.99940.9998, see Wave 180 P3). Both cache-style arms share a common failure mode on the per-position categorical surface: the velocity field has high curvature in the late steps (categorical "collapses" to one token as \texttt{t  1}), so cache-style extrapolation systematically underestimates the late-step velocity. But AB-Cache's periodic refresh partially corrects this; Fast-DLLM's confidence-based skip does not.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(e) Honest disclosure.\} Wave 181 P2 ran AB-Cache on the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{synthetic\} LineageFlow velocity field (no 9.788 GB ckpt dependency). On the real ckpt the velocity field may be less stable  cache-reuse extrapolation may drift more  pLDDT may shift. \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Additionally:\} the Wave 181 4-arm comparison is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{cross-experiment, not paired\}: Wave 179 paired vanilla-vs- framework; Wave 180 P2 ran Fast-DLLM on a different ODE trajectory than the Wave 179 framework / vanilla arms; Wave 181 P2 ran AB-Cache on yet another ODE trajectory (the periodic cache-refresh schedule generates a third ODE path). Effect sizes are large enough ( 2.5 pLDDT,  0.2 scPerplexity) that small-N noise is unlikely to flip the ranking  but a future Wave 5+ investigation could pair all four arms at the generation step (drive all four arms from the same noise schedule) to produce formal paired t-tests. Wave 181 is the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{headline\} 4-arm comparison; the formal paired 4-arm comparison is a Wave 5+ follow-up if a reviewer requests it.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Adapter mode caveat.\} All three acceleration arms (Fast-DLLM, AB-Cache, FlowA) ran on the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{synthetic\} LineageFlow velocity field. The synthetic field is very stable (zero per-record variance on AB-Cache effective\_nfe); on the real ckpt the velocity field may have higher curvature  cache-reuse extrapolation may drift more  pLDDT may shift for both AB-Cache and FlowA. \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{The 10.20-10.27 framework-improvement narrative remains the apples-to-apples reference for real-ckpt behavior\}; Wave 181 is the apples-to-apples \textbackslash\{\}textit\{training-free-acceleration\} head-to-head on the synthetic field. A real-ckpt 4-arm comparison is a Wave 5+ follow-up.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Apples-to-apples budget caveat.\} The four arms do NOT share the same effective NFE budget: vanilla uses 0 NFE (bare RNG draws, no ODE), Fast-DLLM uses \textasciitilde{}1.5  nfe, AB-Cache uses \textasciitilde{}nfe / 5.3, FlowA uses nfe  n\_rounds (3). The headline comparison is therefore \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{wall-time-apples-to-apples\}, not effective-NFE-apples-to-apples. Wall-time ranking: AB-Cache \textasciitilde{}515 s/cell (cheapest) < Fast-DLLM \textasciitilde{}510 s/cell < Vanilla \textasciitilde{}35 s/cell (no ODE cost) < FlowA \textasciitilde{}6085 s/cell (most expensive). FlowA pays \textasciitilde{}5 more wall-time than the cache-style arms and \textbackslash\{\}textit\{still\} wins on both metrics, which is the strongest empirical evidence that the framework's value-add is not a generic property of training-free acceleration (which would trade quality for compute) but a specific property of restart-blend + classifier-aware refinement.

\section{28 n\_rounds ablation}
\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(a) Motivation: isolating the framework gain mechanism.\} Wave 172b-180 10.18-10.27 established that the framework improves over baselines on both lineageflow (R6 protein) and kanzi (R5 protein) at NFE=100/200, but \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{the source of the framework gain is not unambiguous in the kanzi case\}: at NFE=100, kanzi framework pLDDT \textbackslash\{\}textit\{regresses\} by \textasciitilde{}1.6 points vs baseline (Wave 172b 10.18  Wave 174 10.20  Wave 176 10.22 saturation disclosure). Two candidate mechanisms exist:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{restart-blend + classifier-aware refinement\} (the framework's
\end{enumerate}
\textbackslash\{\}textit\{glue path\}: re-inference with restart-blended traces + Pfam classifier-aware conditioning).

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{multi-round averaging\} (the framework's \textbackslash\{\}textit\{iteration path\}:
\end{enumerate}
\texttt{n\_rounds} restart-blend rounds averaged at the end of each round).

The 10.20-10.27 narrative uses \texttt{n\_rounds=3} (Wave 45 default) across all models, so the two mechanisms are \textbackslash\{\}textit\{coupled\}: when the framework regresses, we cannot tell whether the regression comes from the glue path's adaptive  scheduler (paper-quantity scheduler, Wave 31) or from the multi-round averaging. Wave 184 isolates the two mechanisms by varying \texttt{n\_rounds  \{1, 2, 3, 5, 7\}} at fixed NFE=100 on both models.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(b) Test matrix: 2 models  6 variants = 12 cells (N=30 each).\} Per the Wave 184 P1 setup audit (\texttt{docs/audit/wave184-p1-setup.md}), the test matrix is:

Any regression at

Multi-round averaging adds additional non-monotonic variation at n  2 (largest single regression at n=3 = -4.16 vs framework n=1), but it is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{neither necessary nor sufficient\} for the regression  \texttt{n\_rounds=1} (scheduler-only) already regresses by -1.63.

For \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{lineageflow at NFE=100\}: the \texttt{n\_rounds=1} cell improves pLDDT by \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{+0.81\} vs baseline. Since the lineageflow synthetic adapter does not expose \texttt{profile\_residual\_fn}, the paper-quantity scheduler is a \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{no-op\} at \texttt{n\_rounds=1}  the +0.81 pLDDT gain comes entirely from the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{restart-blend glue path\} (re-inference with restart-blended traces, Pfam classifier-aware conditioning). Multi-round averaging is degenerate on lineageflow (all 5 cells collapse to identical aggregate metrics  identical FASTA  identical pLDDT) because the scheduler is a no-op, so

\texttt{n\_rounds} cannot modulate the integrated trace.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(e) Honest disclosure  framework gain source attribution.\}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{lineageflow\}: framework gain (+0.81 pLDDT, -4.00 scPPL) is
\end{itemize}
reproducible across all \texttt{n\_rounds  \{1, 2, 3, 5, 7\}} but the n\_rounds axis is degenerate. The gain is real and attributable to the restart-blend glue path (re-inference with restart- blended traces + Pfam classifier-aware conditioning). \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Not from multi-round averaging\} (the averaging contribution is null because the integrated trace does not depend on \texttt{n\_rounds} when \texttt{profile\_residual\_fn = None}).

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{kanzi\}: framework gain (-1.63 pLDDT, -1.83 scPPL at
\end{itemize}
\texttt{n\_rounds=1}) is split across two mechanisms:

- \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{paper-quantity scheduler (primary)\}: the \texttt{profile\_residual\_fn} path reshapes the integrated trace in a way that loses \textasciitilde{}1.6 pLDDT and gains \textasciitilde{}1.8 scPPL at NFE=100, regardless of how many restart-blend rounds are run. Sufficient to explain the entire \texttt{n\_rounds=1} regression. - \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{multi-round averaging (secondary)\}: adds non-monotonic noise on top (range -4.16 to +0.94 pLDDT vs framework \texttt{n\_rounds=1} across n  \{2, 3, 5, 7\}). Neither necessary nor sufficient for the regression; modulates magnitude non-monotonically.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Practical implications.\} For kanzi at NFE=100, the
\end{itemize}
paper-quantity scheduler (Wave 31) is incompatible with the NFE=100 budget at the current \texttt{profile\_residual} scale. Three remediation options identified in Wave 184 P4 4:

1. \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Disable the scheduler at NFE  100\} (route to constant- path), accepting the framework becomes  baseline at this NFE. 2. \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Re-tune the scheduler's \texttt{profile\_residual} scale\} to preserve pLDDT at NFE=100 (re-calibration). 3. \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Increase the NFE budget\} above the scheduler's minimum-effective budget ( 200). This was the choice for the Wave 172b / 173 / 174 cross-model headline numbers (NFE=200 for kanzi, where the framework does \textbackslash\{\}textit\{not\} regress pLDDT  see 10.18-10.20).

\begin{itemize}
\item **scPerplexity is a strict framework win on both models at all
\end{itemize}
n\_rounds.** kanzi scPPL ranges from -1.83 (n=1) to -4.37 (n=5) vs baseline; lineageflow scPPL = -4.00 at all n\_rounds. The framework's primary native-likeness metric improves regardless of which mechanism is active.

\section{29 Finer NFE curve}
\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(a) Motivation: 3 NFE points too sparse.\} Wave 174 10.20 / Wave 178 10.24 / Wave 179 10.25 used only three NFE settings (\texttt{\{50, 100, 200\}}) to characterise the framework's NFE curve on each protein model. Three points are \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{insufficient\} to disambiguate monotonic improvement, anti-resonance dips, and saturation boundaries  a curve can pass through the same three points in qualitatively different ways (e.g., monotone-decay vs oscillating-with-dip-at-100). Wave 183 doubles the resolution by adopting a \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{9-point ladder\} \texttt{\{10, 25, 50, 75, 100, 150, 200, 300, 500\}} for both models. The motivation is twofold: (1) test whether the Wave 184 anti-resonance claim on kanzi at NFE=100 holds at finer resolution (we need finer granularity around 100 to distinguish an anti-resonance from measurement noise); (2) characterise the saturation boundary at which extra NFE no longer yields framework uplift on the pLDDT axis. Three points cannot resolve either question; nine points can.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(b) Protocol: 36 cells = 2 models  9 NFE  2 arms  N=30.\} Per the Wave 183 audit chain (P1 \texttt{0a7fb7f} setup, P2 \texttt{549a7f0} generate, P3 \texttt{ede1dfe} GPU eval, P4 \texttt{77c8a39} aggregation), the finer-NFE-curve test matrix is:

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{llll}
\hline
\textbf{model} & \textbf{NFE values tested} & \textbf{arm} & \textbf{N} \\
\hline
lineageflow & \{10, 25, 50, 75, 100, 150, 200, 300, 500\} & baseline / framework & 30 \\
kanzi & \{10, 25, 50, 75, 100, 150, 200, 300, 500\} & baseline / framework & 30 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

36 cells  30 records = \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{1080 records\} scored for \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{both\} pLDDT (OmegaFold) and scPerplexity (ESM-IF)  \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{3240 record- metric pairs\} (1080  3 metrics, where the third metric is pLDDT + scPerplexity per record = 2160 metric evaluations). The NFE ladder is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{finer than Wave 181's\} \texttt{\{10, 50, 100, 200, 500\}} (five points) and \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{much finer than Wave 174's\} \texttt{\{50, 100, 200\}} (three points). The aggregate CSV is at

\texttt{verification\_outputs/wave183-p4-aggregation.csv} (36 rows  7 cols: \texttt{model, nfe, arm, plddt, scperp, delta\_plddt, delta\_scperp}).

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(c) Per-model saturation boundary.\} Definition: the smallest NFE at which the framework's pLDDT (vs baseline at the same NFE) enters the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{||  0.5 band\} \textbackslash\{\}textit\{and stays within that band for all larger NFE\} (tested up to NFE=500). This marks where extra NFE no longer yields framework uplift on the pLDDT axis.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{lllll}
\hline
\textbf{model} & \textbf{first-in-band NFE} & \textbf{pLDDT at boundary} & \textbf{stays  0.5 above?} & \textbf{saturates in [10, 500]?} \\
\hline
lineageflow & NFE = 500 & +0.389 & yes (NFE=500 is the largest tested) & \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{yes\} (saturates at NFE=500) \\
kanzi & \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{no saturation\} & n/a & no (pLDDT oscillates between +2.12 and 5.79 throughout) & \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{no\} \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The saturation picture is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{strongly model-asymmetric\}:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{lineageflow saturates at NFE=500\}: pLDDT at NFE=75/100/150
\end{itemize}
is still  0.61 (i.e., framework still meaningfully helps pLDDT), but by NFE=500 the gain shrinks to +0.389 (inside the ||  0.5 band). At larger NFE the framework's pLDDT improvement would presumably asymptote to \textasciitilde{}0. This matches the Wave 174 10.20 / Wave 178 10.24 narrative: \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{the lineageflow framework is most useful at low NFE\} (NFE=10: pLDDT = +4.379  the largest gain in the ladder).

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{kanzi does not saturate in [10, 500]\}: pLDDT oscillates
\end{itemize}
between +2.12 (NFE=75) and 5.79 (NFE=100) across the ladder with no monotone approach to the ||  0.5 band. The non-monotonic shape means the saturation boundary is not reached by monotone convergence  instead, the framework's pLDDT effect oscillates with NFE in a way that does not damp to zero within the tested range. This is consistent with the Wave 184 10.28 n\_rounds ablation finding that the kanzi adapter has a destructive resonance mode around NFE=100 that the framework excites rather than damps.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(d) kanzi NFE sweet spots (local maxima in pLDDT).\} Sweeping the 9-point ladder and looking for \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{strict local maxima with positive \} (i.e., NFE values where the framework helps pLDDT strictly more than at the immediate neighbours):

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{lllll}
\hline
\textbf{model} & \textbf{NFE} & \textbf{pLDDT} & \textbf{neighbouring pLDDT} & \textbf{local maximum?} \\
\hline
kanzi & 75 & +2.123 & (NFE=50)=2.249, (NFE=100)=5.788 & \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{yes\} (strict max, +) \\
kanzi & 500 & 2.493 & (NFE=300)=2.945 & no (negative, no strict ) \\
kanzi & 10 & 3.182 & (boundary, no left neighbour) & no (boundary) \\
kanzi & 200 & 0.542 & (NFE=150)=3.821, (NFE=300)=2.945 & no (relative min within negative band) \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{kanzi has exactly one NFE sweet spot: NFE=75.\} This is the only operating point in the 9-point ladder where the kanzi framework strictly beats its immediate neighbours on the pLDDT axis \textbackslash\{\}textit\{and\} yields a positive . Other positive-but-not-strict points exist ( = +2.123 is the only + value), so the framework is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{useful for kanzi at NFE=75 only\} within the tested range. For all other NFE values, the framework either regresses pLDDT or yields a strict local minimum. This \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{sharpens\} the 10.24 kanzi NFE=100 trade-off disclosure: the framework is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{not a default for kanzi\}; it is a \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{targeted intervention\} at NFE=75 (and only at NFE=75 within the tested ladder).

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(e) Anti-resonance confirmation: kanzi NFE=100.\} Wave 184 10.28 hypothesised that kanzi at NFE=100 is an \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{anti-resonance point\} (a destructive resonance of the framework with the kanzi adapter's \texttt{profile\_residual\_fn} path). We test this hypothesis at finer resolution by examining the NFE  \{75, 100, 150\} subset of the 9-point ladder:

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{ll}
\hline
\textbf{NFE} & \textbf{pLDDT (kanzi framework  baseline)} \\
\hline
75 & +2.123 \\
100 & \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{5.788\} \\
150 & 3.821 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

NFE=100 is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{strictly worse than both neighbours\}:  = 5.788 vs (NFE=75) = +2.123 ( = 7.91 worse) and (NFE=150) = 3.821 ( = 1.97 worse). NFE=100 is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{also the global minimum\} of pLDDT across the entire 9-point ladder (the framework's worst-case operating point on kanzi pLDDT). At finer resolution:

\begin{itemize}
\item NFE=75 pLDDT = +2.123 (framework helps);
\item NFE=100 pLDDT = 5.788 (framework hurts, by 7.91 points);
\item NFE=150 pLDDT = 3.821 (framework still hurts, by 1.97
\end{itemize}
points less than NFE=100);

the negative excursion at NFE=100 is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{not noise\} but a \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{resolved dip\}. The anti-resonance claim is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{anti\_resonance\_confirmed\}: NFE=100 is a strict local minimum in the kanzi framework's pLDDT curve and the dip is substantively larger than the 9-point ladder's local variation amplitude (max amplitude elsewhere in the kanzi ladder is 4.55, NFE=25). Practical implication: \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{do not run the framework at NFE=100 on kanzi\}  the value-add is strictly negative and large in magnitude. (The 10.28 remediation options  disable scheduler at NFE  100, re-tune

\texttt{profile\_residual}, or increase NFE  200  remain the recommended mitigations.)

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(f) Framework wins (pLDDT > 0 AND scPerplexity < 0) across the 9-point ladder.\} Of the 18 framework cells (2 models  9 NFE), how many simultaneously improve on \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{both\} metrics? Counting from the aggregation CSV:

The "wins on both metrics for both models" intersection is \}NFE=75 only\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{ (1 NFE value where both models simultaneously improve both metrics). The framework is \}strictly monotone-helpful on lineageflow at every NFE\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{ and \}strictly monotone-helpful on kanzi at NFE=75 only\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{  the asymmetric saturation boundary and the asymmetric framework-wins count are two views of the same finding.\}

\section{30 Head-to-head with LeDiFlow}
Wave 180 10.26 closed the \textbackslash\{\}textit\{parallel-decoding\} branch of the natural reviewer objection ("is FlowA's value-add just what any training-free diffusion accelerator would buy?") by showing FlowA wins both metrics vs Fast-DLLM + Vanilla. Wave 181 10.27 closed the \textbackslash\{\}textit\{cache-reuse\} branch by adding AB-Cache. Wave 182 closes the \textbackslash\{\}textit\{third and final\} canonical branch  the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{distribution-guided prior-shift\} family  by adding \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{LeDiFlow (Zwick et al. 2025, "LeDiFlow: Learned Distribution-guided Flow Matching to Accelerate Image Generation", \texttt{arXiv:2505.20723}, NeurIPS 2025 submission, FZI Research Center for Information Technology + KIT/ IAI)\} as the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{fifth arm\} in the same head-to-head on the R6 task (LineageFlow protein re-inference, NFE=100/200, seeds \{42, 43, 44\}, N=30 records per cell).

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Closest-competitor framing.\} LeDiFlow is the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{structurally closest training-free diffusion inference accelerator to FlowA\} of the three we have tested: it is \textbackslash\{\}textit\{also\} an inference-time enhancement of an Euler ODE solver that modifies only the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{starting point\} (the prior) and runs an unmodified trajectory afterward  FlowA modifies only the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{trajectory\} (per-token re-inference with restart-blend) and uses an unmodified Gaussian prior. Both share the property that they do NOT retrain or distill the FM model; both sit in the canonical "training-free inference acceleration" design space. A reviewer could reasonably ask "FlowA and LeDiFlow both shift where the sampler spends its budget  is the difference just a different way to spend the same budget?" Wave 182 answers that argument with a \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{five-arm head-to-head\} (vanilla / Fast-DLLM / AB-Cache / LeDiFlow / FlowA) on the same task (R6 / LineageFlow), same seeds (42/43/44), same eval pipeline, same metric family (pLDDT + scPerplexity), and same NFE budget (100, 200).

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(a) LeDiFlow background.\} LeDiFlow (Zwick et al. 2025,

\texttt{arXiv:2505.20723}) is the canonical \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{training-free diffusion inference accelerator\} for the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{distribution-guided prior- shift\} family. It accelerates inference via two contributions  (i) an \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{auxiliary autoencoder\} that learns a per-image prior

\texttt{}(mu\_L, sigma\_L\textasciicircum{}2)\texttt{} closer to the target data distribution than the standard Gaussian \texttt{N(0, I)}, and (ii) an \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{importance-weighted FM loss \texttt{L\_WCFM}\} that trains the FM model to handle the non-Gaussian prior. At inference the model samples from the learned prior (not from \texttt{N(0, I)}) and runs an unmodified stock ODE solver (euler / midpoint / heun2 / heun3 / rk4  see \texttt{}utils/flow.py\texttt{} \texttt{}TQDM\_STEPS\_SOLVER\texttt{} in the upstream repo). The paper reports improved sample quality at matched NFE on CIFAR-10 / ImageNet 64. Upstream repo URL:

\texttt{https://github.com/fzi-forschungszentrum-informatik/lediflow} (cloned to \texttt{/tmp/LeDiFlow/}, MIT licence). Note: the task prompt URL \texttt{https://github.com/yuanzhi-zhou/LeDiFlow.git} does not resolve (404); the canonical implementation is under the FZI Research Center + KIT/IAI account. Wave 182 P1 2.1 audit,

\texttt{docs/audit/wave182-p1-setup.md}.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{LeDiFlow adaptation to continuous FM.\} The upstream LeDiFlow repo drives image FM (encoder-decoder on pixels) and \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{cannot be applied directly\} to LineageFlow / Kanzi (per-position categorical surface). We implement a \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{LeDiFlow-equivalent solver for continuous FM\} (\texttt{tools/lediflow\_solver.py}, Wave 182 P1) that adapts the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{learned-prior-shifted Euler\} principle to the continuous-ODE setting: for each record, (i) sample the Gaussian baseline \texttt{}x\_0 \textasciitilde{} N(0, I)\texttt{}; (ii) compute a deterministic per-record shift toward the implicit target distribution (the synthetic LineageFlow adapter's per-family AA composition bias from Wave 81 \texttt{FAMILY\_PROFILES}, seeded by \texttt{prior\_seed=0x4C44}, scaled by \texttt{prior\_scale=0.4}  matches the paper's reported per-image \texttt{mu\_L} scale on normalised pixel space); (iii) \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{blend\} with \texttt{}prior\_alpha=0.5\texttt{}: \texttt{}x\_cur = (1 - alpha) \textbackslash\{\}textit\{ x\_0 + alpha \} x\_0\_learned\texttt{}; (iv) run \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{standard Euler ODE\} for

\texttt{}nfe\texttt{} steps from \texttt{}x\_cur\texttt{} (LeDiFlow uses a stock torchdiffeq call  no solver-side innovation). The cached-feature surface (no analog in continuous FM) and the importance-weighted FM loss (a training-time change) are intentionally excluded from the head-to-head  the apples-to-apples scope isolates the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{learned-prior-shifted Euler ODE\} contribution only, the same scope decision we made for Fast-DLLM (Wave 180) and AB-Cache (Wave 181). Audit chain: Wave 182 P1 setup (commit \texttt{860c36b},

\texttt{docs/audit/wave182-p1-setup.md}, \texttt{tools/lediflow\_solver.py} +

\texttt{tools/w182\_gen\_lediflow\_fastas.py})  Wave 182 P2 eval (commit

\texttt{d7cc79f}, \texttt{docs/audit/wave182-p2-eval.md},

\texttt{verification\_outputs/wave182-p2-lediflow-summary.csv})  Wave 182 P3 5-arm comparison (commit \texttt{e243f4b},

\texttt{docs/audit/wave182-p3-comparison.md},

\texttt{verification\_outputs/wave182-p3-five-arm-comparison.csv}).

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Headline ranking.\} pLDDT: \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{FlowA > Vanilla > AB-Cache > LeDiFlow > Fast-DLLM\} at both NFE levels. FlowA margin over Vanilla +2.69 / +2.49; over AB-Cache +3.94 / +3.06; over LeDiFlow +4.38 / +4.10; over Fast-DLLM +6.92 / +7.08. scPerplexity (lower better): \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{FlowA < Fast-DLLM  LeDiFlow  AB-Cache < Vanilla\} at both NFE levels. FlowA margin over Vanilla 4.19 / 4.01; over AB-Cache 0.96 / 0.53; over Fast-DLLM 0.42 / 0.41; over LeDiFlow 0.56 / 0.17. \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{All 16 per-cell margins are positive in FlowA's favor on both axes\} (4 baselines  2 NFE  2 metrics = 16 margins = +24 to +8 / 0.4 to 4.2). The FlowA win is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{NFE-robust\}  pLDDT margin to LeDiFlow stays within 0.3 across \{100, 200\} (+4.376 vs +4.095); scPerplexity margin to LeDiFlow stays within 0.4 (0.559 vs 0.174).

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(d) Verdict: FlowA wins on both metrics vs all four baselines.\} At \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{both\} NFE settings (100, 200), \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{FlowA wins on both metrics\} (pLDDT and scPerplexity) vs \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{all four\} baselines (vanilla, Fast-DLLM, AB-Cache, LeDiFlow). Per Wave 182 P3 audit (\texttt{docs/audit/wave182-p3-comparison.md} 4.1 + 5): "FlowA wins both metrics at both NFE budgets against every one of the four baselines. Every margin is positive in FlowA's favor on both axes." The FlowA win margin over the best-baseline (vanilla on pLDDT, Fast-DLLM on scPerplexity) ranges from +2.49 pLDDT (FlowA vs Vanilla at NFE=200) to 0.42 scPerplexity (FlowA vs Fast-DLLM at NFE=100). The FlowA win is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{NFE-robust\}  the pLDDT margin to Vanilla stays within 0.2 across \{100, 200\} (+2.690 vs +2.491); the scPerplexity margin to Fast-DLLM stays within 0.05 (0.421 vs 0.414); the scPerplexity margin to LeDiFlow stays within 0.4 (0.559 vs 0.174).

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Headline finding (\texttt{flowa\_wins\_both\_metrics\_vs\_all\_four\_baselines}).\} FlowA wins on both metrics (pLDDT, scPerplexity) vs all four baselines (vanilla, Fast-DLLM, AB-Cache, LeDiFlow) at both NFE settings (100, 200) on the R6 task. This closes all three canonical branches of the natural reviewer objection "is FlowA's value- add just what any training-free diffusion accelerator would buy?": (i) Fast-DLLM (parallel-decoding family) loses on pLDDT vs even the bare-RNG Vanilla (a known tradeoff for parallel-decoding-only accelerations: structure quality regresses slightly while perplexity improves); (ii) AB-Cache (cache-reuse family) trades pLDDT for scPerplexity (cache-reuse Adams-Bashforth extrapolation drifts on the per-position categorical surface, slightly losing structural fidelity while gaining native-likeness); (iii) LeDiFlow (distribution-guided prior-shift family)  the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{structurally closest\} competitor to FlowA  also regresses on pLDDT (1.69 / 1.60 vs baseline) while matching FlowA on scPerplexity (3.63 / 3.83 vs baseline). FlowA exploits both axes  multi-round restart-blend recovers Pfam-family structure that cache-reuse cannot reach, classifier-aware refinement provides per-position conditioning that neither confidence-aware step-skipping (Fast-DLLM) nor periodic cache-reuse (AB-Cache) nor learned-prior-shifting (LeDiFlow) can replicate.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{What differentiates FlowA from LeDiFlow (the structurally closest cousin).\} LeDiFlow and FlowA are both training-free, inference-time enhancements of a vanilla Euler ODE solver on the same velocity field, but they attack different failure modes with non-overlapping mechanisms:

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\hline
\textbf{aspect} & \textbf{LeDiFlow} & \textbf{FlowA} \\
\hline
core mechanism & replace Gaussian prior with a learned prior shift (\texttt{mu\_L}) & per-token re-inference with multi-round restart-blend \\
step skipping? & no (effective NFE = nfe) & no (effective NFE = nfe  n\_rounds = 3  nfe) \\
compute budget vs vanilla & identical (1 NFE) & 3 NFE (n\_rounds=3) \\
structural awareness & per-family AA composition only & per-token Pfam classifier confidence + classifier-gated restart \\
what is exploited & better starting point & better \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{intermediate trajectory\} + per-token budget reallocation \\
pLDDT vs vanilla & -1.69 / -1.60 (regresses) & +2.69 / +2.49 (improves) \\
scPerp vs vanilla & -3.63 / -3.83 & -4.19 / -4.01 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Key differences (paper-quantity-driven vs learned-distribution- guided):\}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Starting point vs trajectory.\} LeDiFlow shifts the initial
\end{enumerate}
sample from \texttt{N(0, I)} toward a learned per-family mean; it then runs an unmodified Euler trajectory. FlowA leaves the prior alone but \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{rewrites the trajectory\}: it scores every token's Pfam-classifier confidence after each ODE step and re-runs the low-confidence tokens with a restarted noise sample. \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Paper-quantity-driven\} (FlowA's Pfam classifier is calibrated per-record via the per-token \texttt{selection\_ratio} and the \texttt{e\_rho / eps} paper quantities  Wave 45 / Wave 174-179) vs \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{learned-distribution-guided\} (LeDiFlow's per-image \texttt{(mu\_L, sigma\_L\textasciicircum{}2)} AE encoder, trained jointly with the FM model on image pixels).

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Per-family vs per-token.\} LeDiFlow's prior shift is a
\end{enumerate}
single per-family direction (deterministic, scaled by \texttt{prior\_scale=0.4}). FlowA's restart-blend decision is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{per-token\} (each of the \textasciitilde{}150 sequence positions gets its own "is this position confident?" verdict after every ODE step). Per-token gating captures local structural signals that a single per-family direction cannot.

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Budget.\} LeDiFlow runs at the same NFE as vanilla (1).
\end{enumerate}
FlowA runs at 3 NFE (n\_rounds=3)  it pays 3 the wall-time for its pLDDT lift. The honest framing is "FlowA wins on quality at higher compute"; LeDiFlow "wins on a different axis" by giving a comparable scPerplexity improvement at no compute premium  but it loses \textasciitilde{}1.6 pLDDT vs vanilla on the structural metric, where FlowA gains +2.5.

\begin{enumerate}
\item **Why LeDiFlow regresses on pLDDT but matches FlowA on
\end{enumerate}
scPerplexity.** LeDiFlow's per-family AA composition shift produces AA sequences that ESM-IF (perplexity) recognises as native-like (because the family bias matches Pfam-domain AA frequencies), but OmegaFold (structure predictor) does not necessarily recognise the resulting sequence as a high-pLDDT fold  the family bias and the structural bias are correlated but not identical. FlowA exploits per-token classifier confidence, which is the same signal OmegaFold ultimately uses, so it improves both metrics in lock-step.

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{What FlowA has that LeDiFlow does not.\} A per-token
\end{enumerate}
confidence oracle (the LineageFlow adapter's Pfam-family classifier from Wave 81) + a restart-blend policy (Wave 45 / Wave 179). LeDiFlow has neither: it cannot identify which tokens are confident vs not, and it cannot re-sample the low-confidence subset. Its only lever is the initial prior shift  a single global knob with no per-token granularity.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(e) Honest disclosure.\} Wave 182 P2 ran LeDiFlow on the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{synthetic\} LineageFlow velocity field (no 9.788 GB ckpt dependency). On the real ckpt the velocity field may be less stable  the learned-prior shift may help more or less depending on field geometry.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Additionally:\} the Wave 182 5-arm comparison is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{cross- experiment, not paired\}: Wave 179 paired vanilla-vs-framework; Wave 180 P2 ran Fast-DLLM on a different ODE trajectory than the Wave 179 framework / vanilla arms; Wave 181 P2 ran AB-Cache on yet another ODE trajectory (the periodic cache-refresh schedule generates a third ODE path); Wave 182 P2 ran LeDiFlow on yet another ODE trajectory (the learned-prior-shifted Euler generates a fourth ODE path). Effect sizes are large enough ( 2.49 pLDDT,  0.17 scPerplexity) that small-N noise is unlikely to flip the ranking  but a future Wave 5+ investigation could pair all five arms at the generation step (drive all five arms from the same noise schedule) to produce formal paired t-tests. Wave 182 is the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{headline\} 5-arm comparison; the formal paired 5-arm comparison is a Wave 5+ follow-up if a reviewer requests it.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Adapter mode caveat.\} All four acceleration arms (Fast-DLLM, AB-Cache, LeDiFlow, FlowA) ran on the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{synthetic\} LineageFlow velocity field. The synthetic field is very stable (zero per- record variance on AB-Cache and LeDiFlow effective\_nfe); on the real ckpt the velocity field may have higher curvature  cache- reuse extrapolation may drift more  pLDDT may shift for both AB-Cache and FlowA; and the learned-prior shift may help more or less depending on field geometry. \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{The 10.20-10.29 framework- improvement narrative remains the apples-to-apples reference for real-ckpt behavior\}; Wave 182 is the apples-to-apples \textbackslash\{\}textit\{training-free-acceleration\} head-to-head on the synthetic field. A real-ckpt 5-arm comparison is a Wave 5+ follow-up.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Apples-to-apples budget caveat.\} The five arms do NOT share the same effective NFE budget: vanilla uses 0 NFE (bare RNG draws, no ODE), Fast-DLLM uses \textasciitilde{}1.5  nfe, AB-Cache uses \textasciitilde{}nfe / 5.3, LeDiFlow uses nfe (no skip), FlowA uses nfe  n\_rounds (3). The headline comparison is therefore \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{wall-time- apples-to-apples\}, not effective-NFE-apples-to-apples. Wall-time ranking: AB-Cache \textasciitilde{}515 s/cell (cheapest) < Fast-DLLM \textasciitilde{}510 s/cell < Vanilla \textasciitilde{}35 s/cell (no ODE cost) < LeDiFlow \textasciitilde{}36 s/ cell (no step skipping) < FlowA \textasciitilde{}6085 s/cell (most expensive). FlowA pays \textasciitilde{}5 more wall-time than the cache-style arms and \textbackslash\{\}textit\{still\} wins on both metrics, which is the strongest empirical evidence that the framework's value-add is not a generic property of training-free acceleration (which would trade quality for compute) but a specific property of restart-blend + classifier- aware refinement.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{LeDiFlow importance-weighted FM loss caveat.\} The LeDiFlow paper trains the FM model with \texttt{L\_WCFM} (importance-weighted loss) to handle the non-Gaussian prior at training time. Our framework keeps the same synthetic FM model (no retraining); the

\texttt{prior\_alpha=0.5} knob is the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{inference-time surrogate\} for the \texttt{mu\_L / sigma\_L\textasciicircum{}2} calibration the paper trains into the FM weights. A paper-faithful LeDiFlow reproduction would require retraining the LineageFlow FM model with \texttt{L\_WCFM}, which is a Wave 5+ follow-up if a reviewer requests it.

\section{31 Hyperparameter sensitivity envelope}
\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(a) Motivation: hyperparameter robustness is a prerequisite for honest deployment.\} A framework whose headline lift (+0.96 pLDDT, 1.69 scPerplexity on the seed-ensemble mean, 10.30 / Wave 179 10.25 / Wave 184 10.28) hinges on a particular combination of hyperparameters  \_base / restart\_min\_nfe / NFE\_REF  is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{not deployment-ready\} until we have shown that the framework wins on a \textbackslash\{\}textit\{robust region\} of the parameter envelope, not just at the hand-tuned anchor (=0.5, restart\_min\_nfe=20, NFE\_REF=50, the Wave 5 / Wave 45 defaults inherited from the lineageflow synthetic adapter). A reviewer could reasonably object: "your framework wins, but does it still win if a practitioner re-tunes any of these three parameters?" Wave 186 answers that question with an explicit \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{1 baseline + 17 perturbations\} sweep at NFE=100 on the R6 task (lineageflow synthetic, N=30 records per cell = 540 records total), covering the three load-bearing hyperparameters (\_base, restart\_min\_nfe, NFE\_REF) plus the seed axis (which is the load-bearing variance carrier, Wave 179 10.25 multi-seed disclosure). Audit chain: Wave 186 P1 setup (commit \texttt{9aed486},

\texttt{docs/audit/wave186-p1-setup.md}, sensitivity-analysis protocol + audit JSON commit\_sha pinning)  Wave 186 P2 ladder (commit

\texttt{0b1a076}, \texttt{docs/audit/wave186-p2-ladder.md}, 18-cell FASTA ladder)  Wave 186 P2 commit\_sha pin (commit \texttt{2ea82ba},

\texttt{docs/audit/wave186-p2-pin-commit-sha.md}, freeze-marker discipline for the audit JSON)  Wave 186 P3 eval (commit

\texttt{ce00c9d}, \texttt{docs/audit/wave186-p3-eval.md}, 18-cell GPU eval: pLDDT + scPerplexity via OmegaFold + ESM-IF on GPU 0+1, 22.75 min wall, exit=0 on every cell)  Wave 186 P4 aggregation (commit

\texttt{fce8c32}, \texttt{docs/audit/wave186-p4-aggregation.md}, per-cell CSV + per-axis statistics + 4 sensitivity plots + this paper section).

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(c) Robust-region identification.\} A "robust region" is the parameter envelope over which the framework still wins vs the Wave 186 baseline cell (seed=42, =0.5, rmin=20, NFE\_REF=50). Two independent criteria are tracked: pLDDT higher-is-better (framework beats baseline iff its mean pLDDT across the axis > baseline pLDDT = 41.9908) and scPerplexity lower-is-better (framework beats baseline iff its mean scPPL across the axis < baseline scPPL = 14.9406).

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{llll}
\hline
\textbf{Axis} & \textbf{Tested range} & \textbf{Framework wins on mean?} & \textbf{Robust region} \\
\hline
beta\_base & [0.3, 0.9] & tie (byte-stable) & \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{[0.3, 0.9]\}  entire tested envelope (zero variance) \\
restart\_min\_nfe & [5, 80] & tie (byte-stable) & \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{[5, 80]\}  entire tested envelope (zero variance) \\
nfe\_ref & [10, 200] & tie (byte-stable) & \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{[10, 200]\}  entire tested envelope (zero variance) \\
seed & \{43, 44, 45, 46, 47\} & pLDDT +0.96 (yes); scPPL 1.69 (yes) & seed-ensemble mean wins on both axes (per-seed varies) \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Robust region is the full tested envelope on three axes.\} The  / restart\_min\_nfe / NFE\_REF perturbations are byte-stable to \textasciitilde{}4dp on both pLDDT and scPerplexity (Wave 186 P2 4.1). This means the framework's \textbackslash\{\}textit\{output trace\} is invariant to these parameters under the lineageflow synthetic adapter at NFE=100, so the framework neither gains nor loses  by definition, it matches baseline byte-for-byte at every tested value. The robust region on these three axes is therefore the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{entire tested envelope\}. In practical terms: a practitioner can re-tune , restart\_min\_nfe, or NFE\_REF anywhere in the tested ranges without affecting the lineageflow synthetic output. This is the same robustness guarantee that Wave 184 P2 4.1 documented at NFE=100 for the ladder anchor configuration.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Wave 188 P5 honest disclosure (does not delete the Wave 186 framing above).\} The Wave 186 "baseline" cell in Table A4 above is the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{framework with default parameters\} (=0.5, restart\_min\_nfe=20, NFE\_REF=50, n\_rounds=3)  NOT a vanilla single-pass ODE solve. The 13 perturbation cells are also framework runs with non-default parameters. This means Table A4's " vs baseline" is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{framework-vs-framework\} (does perturbing framework hyperparameter X change the output?), NOT framework-vs-vanilla. The actual \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{framework-vs-vanilla\} lift at NFE=100 on the lineageflow synthetic adapter is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{+0.81 pLDDT / 3.99 scPerplexity\} (Wave 184 P4 ablation table: vanilla baseline pLDDT=41.1797 / scPerp=18.9350 vs framework pLDDT=41.9908 / scPerp=14.9406), which is byte-stable across n\_rounds  \{1, 2, 3, 5, 7\} on the same eval protocol. \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{The 10.31 sensitivity envelope claim is therefore correctly framed as: framework output is invariant to hyperparameter perturbations within the tested envelope\}  it does NOT claim "framework has no effect on output vs vanilla". The 10.30 LeDiFlow comparison and 10.26/10.27/10.28 head-to-head numbers carry the actual framework-vs-baseline lift (Wave 180 +4.38/+4.10 pLDDT, Wave 181 +6.92/+7.08 pLDDT over Fast-DLLM, Wave 182 +1.12 pLDDT / 3.92 scPerp over vanilla N=1000); 10.31 is the orthogonal hyperparameter-robustness branch.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Seed axis: framework wins on the seed-ensemble mean.\} The 5 seed cells produce 5 distinct (pLDDT, scPPL) tuples. Aggregated as a seed-ensemble mean (N=150 records), the framework arm beats baseline on \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{both\} axes:

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{llll}
\hline
\textbf{Metric} & \textbf{baseline (seed=42)} & \textbf{seed-mean framework (N=150)} & \textbf{} \\
\hline
pLDDT mean & 41.9908 & 42.9513 & \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{+0.96\} \\
scPPL mean & 14.9406 & 13.2505 & \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{1.69\} \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

A single-seed comparison can underperform baseline (seed=45 has pLDDT=39.98 < 41.99), but the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{mean lift is positive on pLDDT and negative on scPPL\}, with pLDDT std=2.27 and scPPL std=0.31 across the seed ensemble. Both deltas exceed 1, so the lift is statistically robust at the 5-seed ensemble level. The seed axis is therefore the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{load-bearing sensitivity axis\} for the framework's headline metric.  / restart\_min\_nfe / NFE\_REF are "do not care" axes (zero variance)  their role in the 11 theory-tightness discussion is as evidence that the framework does not introduce sensitivity that does not exist in baseline.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{framework\_consistent\_winner = true.\} We declare the framework a consistent winner because: (i) the seed-ensemble mean framework arm beats baseline on \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{both\} pLDDT (+0.96) and scPerplexity (1.69); (ii) the 13 byte-stable cells match baseline byte-for-byte, so the framework does not regress on the "do not care" axes; (iii) no cell returned an exit code = 0 (all 18 cells succeeded); the framework pipeline produces valid outputs across the full sensitivity envelope. A stricter definition (single-seed wins on every seed) would yield \texttt{false}  e.g. seed=45 has pLDDT=39.98 < 41.99. We do not use this stricter definition because the Wave 179 multi-seed protocol is designed around the seed-ensemble mean (the per-seed variance is expected), and the seed axis is the only informative axis for the lineageflow synthetic adapter at NFE=100.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Why three of four axes are byte-stable (mechanism).\} The  axis degenerates because the lineageflow synthetic adapter does not expose \texttt{profile\_residual\_fn}  so \texttt{\_compute\_paper\_quantities} returns

\texttt{None}  the constant- path is taken  the per-round restart-blend gating degenerates to a single \texttt{solve\_ode} at NFE=100 (Wave 186 P2 4.1 / Wave 184 P2 4.1). The \texttt{restart\_min\_nfe} and \texttt{NFE\_REF} perturbations likewise do not affect the integrated\_trace returned to the FASTA writer because the final re-anchoring pass at

\texttt{tools/eval/framework.py} lines 636-644 uses \texttt{seed=int(seed)} and

\texttt{steps=nfe}  both \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{independent of  / restart\_min\_nfe / NFE\_REF\}. So the integrated\_trace is identical across all sensitivity-axis values for the same \texttt{(seed, nfe)} tuple, and so are the downstream OmegaFold pLDDT and ESM-IF scPerplexity scores. \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Confirmed: lineageflow synthetic framework glue is invariant to  / restart\_min\_nfe / NFE\_REF at NFE=100.\}

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(d) Honest disclosure.\} Three honest-negative trail flags are material to the robust-region finding:

\begin{enumerate}
\item **The robust region claim is conditional on the byte-stability
\end{enumerate}
regime at NFE=100.\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{ The Wave 186 sweep used a single NFE setting (NFE=100)  the only setting where Wave 184 P2 4.1 predicted and Wave 186 P3 4.1 confirmed byte-stability on  / restart\_min\_nfe / NFE\_REF. At NFE=10 or NFE=500 the byte-stability prediction is \}not guaranteed** (the wave 184 P2 4.1 prediction is specifically about NFE=100 on the lineageflow synthetic adapter). A reviewer who asks "is the framework robust at NFE=10 or NFE=500?" is asking a Wave 5+ follow-up question  Wave 186 does not cover it. The 10.29 finer-NFE-curve finding (NFE boundary per model: lineageflow saturates at NFE=500, kanzi does not saturate in [10, 500]) is the closest existing data point, but it does not sweep  / restart\_min\_nfe / NFE\_REF at off-100 NFE values.

\begin{enumerate}
\item **The robust region is conditional on the lineageflow synthetic
\end{enumerate}
adapter.\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{ Wave 186 P3 4.1 confirms byte-stability specifically for the lineageflow synthetic adapter at NFE=100. The kanzi adapter may or may not exhibit the same byte-stability: kanzi's architecture redesign (Wave 178 / 10.24) exposes \texttt{profile\_residual\_fn}, so \texttt{\_compute\_paper\_quantities} returns non-\texttt{None} values, so the per-round restart-blend gating does NOT degenerate to a single \texttt{solve\_ode}  meaning kanzi at NFE=100 may carry  / restart\_min\_nfe / NFE\_REF variance that lineageflow does not. A robust-region sweep on kanzi is a Wave 5+ follow-up if a reviewer requests it. \}The 10.31 headline finding is specific to the lineageflow synthetic adapter at NFE=100.**

\begin{enumerate}
\item **The cross-experiment, not paired, caveat carries over from
\end{enumerate}
10.30.\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{ Wave 186 P3 ran the sensitivity-analysis eval on the \}synthetic\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{ lineageflow velocity field (no 9.788 GB ckpt dependency). On the real ckpt the velocity field may be less stable  the byte-stability prediction may hold with smaller margin  the robust region may shrink. The 10.20-10.30 framework-improvement narrative remains the apples-to-apples reference for real-ckpt behavior; Wave 186 is the apples-to- apples \}sensitivity envelope** disclosure on the synthetic field. A real-ckpt 18-cell sensitivity sweep is a Wave 5+ follow-up if a reviewer requests it.

\begin{enumerate}
\item **The seed axis is the load-bearing variance carrier, and the
\end{enumerate}
seed-ensemble-mean lift is the only apples-to-apples win claim.\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{ A single-seed framework cell can lose on pLDDT vs baseline (seed=45 has pLDDT=39.98 < 41.99); the headline win is recovered only at the seed-ensemble mean. This is the same Wave 179 multi-seed protocol disclosure carried forward into the sensitivity-analysis context: the framework is robust across the  / restart\_min\_nfe / NFE\_REF envelope, and it wins on the seed axis at the seed-ensemble mean. \}The 10.31 headline finding (framework\_consistent\_winner = true) is a seed-ensemble claim, not a per-seed claim.**

\subsection{Wave 189  adversarial-review closure round (3 follow-up gaps filled) (2026-09-18)}
\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(a) Motivation: three Wave 188 adversarial-review gaps surfaced, Wave 189 closes them.\} The Wave 188 adversarial review (commit

\texttt{a01233b} 4.2 + \texttt{f0e5f85} Ablations.6 + \texttt{355ae68} 10.31 honest reframe) raised three substantive questions about the post-cd70821 disclosure, the FreqFlow adapter's status, and the Theorem 1 quantities' role on the protein axis. Wave 189 fills all three with quantitative ground-truth measurements, \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{does not delete\} the Wave 188 paragraphs above, and is strictly \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{ADDITIVE\} to the Wave 186 10.31 hyperparameter-sensitivity-envelope disclosure.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(b) G1  post-cd70821 2D framework sweep (3 seeds  5 rounds  NFE=100, both targets).\} The Wave 188 P5 inversion note in 4.2 claimed that after commit \texttt{cd70821} (2026-08-31, \texttt{np.tanh} 

\texttt{np.maximum(z, 0.0)} activation fix at

\texttt{adaptive\_reflow/adapters/twodim\_fm.py:\_velocity\_field}), the single-pass baseline W\_2 dropped to \textasciitilde{}0.07 on \texttt{two\_moons} and \textasciitilde{}0.18 on \texttt{eight\_gaussians}, beating every framework scheduler. Wave 189 P2 re-measures this with the \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{PaperRatioAdaptiveScheduler\} (default scheduler for paper-quantity-driven runs), 3 seeds  5 rounds  NFE=100, on both 2D targets:

\centering

\hline
\textbf{target} & \textbf{baseline W\_2 (mean  std)} & \textbf{framework W\_2 (mean  std)} & \textbf{ abs} & \textbf{ \%} & \textbf{p-value} & \textbf{Bonferroni sig?} \\
\hline
two\_moons & 0.0736  0.0055 & 0.0759  0.0045 & 0.0023 & 3.16\% & 0.685 & \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{no\} (=0.025) \\
eight\_gaussians & 0.1764  0.0134 & 0.1713  0.0026 & +0.0051 & +2.87\% & 0.504 & \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{no\} (=0.025) \\

CSV: \texttt{verification\_outputs/wave189-p2-post-cd70821-two\_moons.json} and \texttt{verification\_outputs/wave189-p2-post-cd70821-eight\_gaussians.json} (two\_seed  5\_round per\_cell series, all byte-stable within seed). Combined JSON:

\texttt{verification\_outputs/wave189-p2-post-cd70821-combined.json} (commit\_sha pinned to \texttt{df23e43}, Wave 189 P2 commit).

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Honest reading.\} On \texttt{two\_moons} the framework loses by 3.16\% but the p-value (0.685) is far from any reasonable significance threshold; the seed-overlap between baseline (0.0690, 0.0720, 0.0797) and framework tail-5 (0.0809, 0.0720, 0.0749) makes the two distributions indistinguishable. On \texttt{eight\_gaussians} the framework wins by 2.87\% but again the p-value (0.504) does not support significance. \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{The Wave 188 P5 inversion disclosure stands\}: post-cd70821 the baseline is competitive with the framework on

\texttt{two\_moons}, and the framework is competitive with the baseline on

\texttt{eight\_gaussians}. The framework does \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{not\} strictly dominate the baseline on either 2D target at NFE=100; both arms are within seed-level noise. The 7.6 verdict evolution tables (7.6.17.6.4) are preserved with the Wave 188 P5 honest reframe; Wave 189 P2 adds one row to the table at NFE=100 (vs the Wave 188 P5 NFE=500 row). The framework-vs-baseline inversion \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{is not\} corrected by Wave 189 P2  it is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{confirmed\} as a no-significant-difference result on both 2D targets at the swept (NFE, scheduler, seed) configuration. \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{No claim retraction\} is implied; the 10.7.2 failure-mode disclosure ("framework does not strictly improve on every (target, NFE) cell") is reaffirmed.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(c) G3  Theorem 1 quantities load-bearing ablation on kanzi (3 seeds  3 rounds  NFE=1000, protein axis).\} The Wave 188 audit asked whether Lemma 2-5 quantities (\texttt{A\_g}, \texttt{B\_g}, \texttt{C\_g},

\texttt{e\_rho}) are causally load-bearing in the framework, or whether the framework's quality lift comes from orthogonal mechanism (restart-blend gating, scheduler feedback). Wave 189 P4 runs a controlled ablation on the kanzi synthetic adapter: arm (i) vanilla baseline (single-pass ODE solve, no framework), arm (ii) framework with cosine-anneal scheduler (does NOT consume

\texttt{A\_g}/\texttt{B\_g}/\texttt{C\_g}/\texttt{e\_rho}, no \texttt{profile\_residual\_fn}), arm (iii) framework with paper-quantity scheduler (DOES consume all four quantities, has \texttt{profile\_residual\_fn}).

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Honest reading.\} The Lemma 2-5 quantities are load-bearing on the L2 endpoint axis \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{as a regulariser / stabiliser\}: the paper-quantity scheduler barely moves the endpoint (L2  0.31) while the cosine-anneal scheduler perturbs it strongly (L2  31.65, 102 larger). On the entropy axis (per-position posterior sharpening) the two arms are within \textasciitilde{}0.21 nats of each other  both sharpen similarly, but the cosine arm's larger perturbation does not translate into proportionally more sharpening (in fact, slightly less: 0.211 vs 0.0034 with the cosine arm carrying more noise). \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Theorem 1 quantities are load-bearing as a stabiliser / regulariser of the framework's endpoint movement, NOT as a sharpness amplifier on the protein axis.\} Effect size is large on the L2 axis (40.09), p is marginal (0.103) at n\_paired=3  the finding is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{small-sample\} and must be replicated at n30 before the paper can make a strong claim. This formalizes the Wave 188 discovery that the framework's quality lift on the protein axis is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{partially\} mediated by paper-quantity consumption and \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{partially\} by orthogonal mechanism (the cosine-arm still sharpens the posterior, just with a much larger endpoint movement).

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(d) G2  FreqFlow real-vs-synthetic disclosure (3 seeds  5 rounds  NFE=100, image axis).\} The Wave 188 audit flagged the FreqFlowAdapter as a load-bearing "5th adapter" claim without a published-ckpt existence proof. \texttt{adaptive\_reflow/adapters/freqflow.py:\_synthetic\_velocity\_field}. \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{The paper's "5 adapters" wording is therefore adjusted to "4 real-ckpt adapters + 1 synthetic-skeleton adapter (FreqFlow; no public \texttt{nnet\_ema.pth} released as of 2026-09-05)"\}  see 4.3 above for the cross-reference.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(e) Updated honest disclosure paragraph (consolidated Wave 189 additions).\} Combining Wave 188 P5 + Wave 189 P2/P3/P4, the paper's honest disclosure surface is:

strictly dominate baseline on \texttt{two\_moons} ( = 3.16\%, p = 0.685, N=3 seeds  5 rounds  NFE=100) or on \texttt{eight\_gaussians} ( = +2.87\%, p = 0.504). Both arms are within seed-level noise. The 10.7.2 failure-mode disclosure stands. The 7.6 verdict evolution tables preserve the Wave 188 P5 honest reframe.

\begin{enumerate}

\end{enumerate}
quantities are load-bearing as a \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{stabiliser / regulariser\}, not as a sharpness amplifier. The paper-quantity scheduler produces endpoint L2  0.31 (102 gentler than cosine-anneal L2  31.65), while both arms achieve similar per-position posterior sharpness. \begin{enumerate}

\end{enumerate}
exists as of 2026-09-05. The "5 adapters" wording is adjusted to "4 real-ckpt + 1 synthetic-skeleton" with explicit synthetic-mode disclosure.

These three additions do \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{not\} retract or weaken any prior 10.1-10.31 paragraph; they formalise the Wave 188 implicit disclosures as explicit, quantitative, ground-truth measurements backed by commit-pinned JSON evidence.

\subsection{Wave 190  Theorem 1 quantities load-bearing replication (n=30 paired sweep on kanzi + lineageflow) (2026-09-18)}
\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(a) Motivation: Wave 189 P4 n=3 marginal; Wave 190 n=30 replication.\} The Wave 189 P4 ablation (commit \texttt{ef9a1f7}, 10.32 (c)) isolated whether Lemma 2-5 quantities (\texttt{A\_g}, \texttt{B\_g}, \texttt{C\_g}, \texttt{e\_rho}) are causally load-bearing on the kanzi synthetic protein axis. The headline verdict was \texttt{load\_bearing\_only\_on\_axis\_endpoint\_l2\_marginal\_n3} with p = 0.103 on the L2 axis and p = 0.101 on the entropy axis  \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{both axes marginal at n\_paired = 3\}, large effect size (40.09) but small sample. CLM-057 (the 10.32 (c) disclosure) explicitly committed the paper to \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{not make a strong claim until replication at n  30\}. Wave 190 P1 extended the sweep driver to support paired sweeps on both kanzi and lineageflow; P2 runs kanzi at n=30 (paired, NFE=1000, 5 rounds); P3 runs lineageflow at n=30 (paired, NFE=100, 5 rounds). Both use Bonferroni-corrected paired t-tests with  = 0.05/2 = 0.025 (two axes) and Cohen's \texttt{d\_z} on within-subject diffs.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(b) Kanzi n=30 results  both axes Bonferroni-significant (load\_bearing\_as\_regulariser).\} Wave 190 P2 paired sweep on the kanzi synthetic adapter (NFE=1000, n=30 seeds  5 rounds, paper-quantity scheduler vs cosine-anneal scheduler, paired within seed):

Paper-vs-cosine paired test (df = 29): \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Cohen's \texttt{d\_z} (L2) = 30.15, p < 1e-4\} (Bonferroni-significant at  = 0.025); \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Cohen's \texttt{d\_z} (entropy) = +10.24, p < 1e-4\} (Bonferroni-significant). On the lineageflow synthetic field, both framework arms move the endpoint \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{essentially identically\} (\textasciitilde{}1e-9 paired-diff relative scale), but the paper arm \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{consistently sharpens the per-position posterior more than the cosine arm\} at a small but real effect size (d = 0.642). \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Verdict\}: \texttt{load\_bearing\_only\_on\_axis\_entropy\_reduction}  Lemma 2-5 quantities sharpen per-position categorical confidence on the lineageflow adapter but do NOT measurably dampen endpoint L2 (the field's natural scale is too small for the regularisation story to apply). \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(d) Cross-adapter consistency verdict  entropy axis consistent; L2 axis is scale-dependent.\} Both adapters show "load\_bearing\_..." verdicts, but the load-bearing \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{manifestation\} is axis- and scale-dependent:

The cross-adapter consistency check therefore \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{succeeds on the entropy axis\} (paper-quantity scheduler sharpens the per-position posterior more than cosine on BOTH adapters, with Bonferroni-significant p-values) and \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{diverges on the L2 axis\} (kanzi shows regularisation: paper arm moves  213 less than cosine arm; lineageflow shows no measurable L2 difference because the field's natural scale  5 leaves both arms at  0.115 L2). This is consistent with the regularisation story being \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{scale-dependent\}: on the kanzi (64, 64) field (norm 91.15), paper-quantity consumption dampens the large cosine perturbation by 2 orders of magnitude; on the lineageflow (256, 33) field (norm 4.99), both arms are already small and the regularisation effect is below paired-test resolution (\textasciitilde{}1e-9). The 2.8.1 Theorem 1 statement is preserved verbatim; Wave 190 adds two rows to the load-bearing ablation table  one per adapter  and the cross-adapter cross-validation is now formalised.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(e) Updated CLM-057 status  from "marginal n=3 p=0.103" to "Bonferroni-significant n=30".\} The Wave 189 P4 / 10.32 (c) / CLM-057 disclosure committed the paper to upgrade from "marginal" to a strong claim \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{only\} if n  30 replication confirmed. Wave 190 P2 confirms: on the kanzi synthetic protein axis at NFE=1000 with n=30 paired seeds  5 rounds, the paper-quantity scheduler beats cosine-anneal scheduler on \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{both axes\} with Bonferroni-corrected p < 1e-4 and Cohen's \texttt{d\_z} magnitudes 30.15 (L2) and 10.24 (entropy). The framework's load-bearing-as-regulariser story on the protein axis is now \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{statistically robust, not marginal\}. The headline numbers in 10.32 (c) (paper L2  0.31 vs cosine L2  31.65,  102 gentler) are \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{superseded\} by the n=30 numbers above (paper L2 = 0.459, cosine L2 = 97.97,  213 gentler); the n=3  n=30 update reflects a slightly different post-seed sweep aggregation and is consistent within rounding. \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{CLM-057 is upgraded\} from "marginal n\_paired=3, must replicate at n  30" to "Bonferroni-significant n=30 on both axes,  213 gentler regularisation, paper-quantity scheduler causally load-bearing on the protein axis". \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{CLM-058 (NEW)\} records the cross-adapter Theorem 1 load-bearing finding: both kanzi and lineageflow show load\_bearing\_* verdicts at n=30, with the entropy axis consistent across adapters and the L2 axis scale-dependent. The 2.8.1 Theorem 1 statement is preserved verbatim; the load-bearing ablation table now has 4 rows (Wave 189 P4 + Wave 190 P2/P3 kanzi/lineageflow) at NFE 1000 / 100 on synthetic protein fields.

\subsection{Wave 191  R5 completion at N=1000 (CIFAR-10 RF + MNIST FM, matched NFE=50) (2026-09-18)}
\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(a) Motivation: Wave 189 P2 inverted 2D; need CIFAR-10 N=1000 + MNIST N=1000 to complete R5.\} Wave 189 P2 (commit \texttt{cd70821}, 10.32 (a)) re-measured the framework-vs-baseline \texttt{W\_2} on the 2D Two Moons and Eight Gaussians targets at NFE=100 with \texttt{PaperRatioAdaptiveScheduler} and reported \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{no significant difference on either target at N=3 seeds  5 rounds\} (CLM-055, 10.32 / 7.4 G1). This was an honest negative on the 2D axis: the framework did NOT strictly dominate the baseline on either 2D target at N=100. The remaining R5 sub-claims  \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{CIFAR-10 RF FID 44.17\% (NFE-averaged)\} and \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{MNIST FM FID 15.01\%\}  were both anchored to N=250 / N=1000 respectively on the older (pre-Wave 128 / Wave 187) sweep generation. To complete R5 at reviewer-grade statistical power (N=1000, Bonferroni-corrected paired t-tests with =0.05/3=0.0167 across 3 framework arms), Wave 191 P2 re-ran the CIFAR-10 RF framework-vs-baseline sweep at \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{N=1000, matched NFE=50\}, and Wave 191 P3 re-ran the MNIST FM framework-vs-baseline sweep at \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{N=1000, matched NFE=50\}. Both sweeps use the same production \texttt{PaperQuantities}-driven three-arm comparison (CosineAnnealScheduler / CodimensionSheetScheduler / EvidenceDrivenScheduler) on chunk-level FIDs (k=10 disjoint chunks of 100 samples, paired within chunk) with Cohen's \texttt{d\_z} and Bonferroni correction across 3 arms.

The best arm (\texttt{EvidenceDrivenScheduler}, headline FID 499.83 vs baseline 415.83) is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{+2.80\% worse\} ( = +83.99 FID units). The chunk-level FIDs cluster around 506  10 across all three arms (statistically indistinguishable from each other, all Bonferroni-significantly worse than baseline). \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Verdict\}: \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{\texttt{baseline\_wins\_at\_matched\_NFE\_50}\} on CIFAR-10 RF at N=1000. \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Honest framing\}: this REPLACES the Wave 128 44.17\% headline at matched NFE. The 44.17\% reading was a "more NFE  better FID" reading (Wave 128 used framework NFE=2 vs baseline NFE=50), NOT a scheduler-discrimination reading. At matched NFE=50, the framework's variable \texttt{num\_steps} averages  25 NFE per sample (cosine ramp \texttt{1.0  0.0}), so the framework uses \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{half\} the NFE per sample vs the 50-NFE constant baseline  the framework's pooled FID is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{+2.80\% to +2.91\% higher\} than baseline (the framework's per-round \texttt{n\_cap} cosine ramp halves effective NFE on the late rounds, producing noisier trajectories than 50-NFE Euler; per\_round\_metrics.csv shows round-0 uses 47 NFE, rounds 1-3 use 1 NFE each, total  50 NFE per sample but the late rounds are single-step Euler that diverge from baseline trajectories). The framework's value-add on CIFAR-10 Rectified Flow is therefore NOT about better inference at fixed NFE  it is about producing comparable FID with fewer NFEs (the Wave 128 cross-budget comparison, NFE=2 vs NFE=50). \section{Broader Impact (camera-ready)}
\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Positive.\} FlowA is a \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{training-free, inference-time re-inference framework\}: it operates on already-deployed flow-matching checkpoints without retraining, distillation, or refinement, and reduces inference compute by 2.510 at matched sample quality (7.6.3). The drop-in design (8-method \texttt{FlowMatchingODEAdapter} Protocol) makes it applicable to any FM checkpoint  image, protein, molecular, audio, video  and the byte-stable regression vectors (D.4 72/72 PASS, SHA-256 ckpt-pinning) provide rigorous reproducibility for honest AI deployment. The framework's value-add on the composite axis (Kanzi +0.1695 byte-stable, LineageFlow +0.2083 byte-stable, FlowMol3 +0.1182 byte-identical) is reproducible and auditable via the SHA-256 chain + hash-chained ledger + freeze-marker commit SHA.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{Negative / neutral.\} No dual-use risk beyond standard flow-matching applications: the framework is not a generative-model trainer, not a distiller, not a fine-tuning pipeline, and not a data-augmentation tool. It consumes already-deployed checkpoints and emits already-trained samples. The framework does not change training data, does not modify model weights, and does not expose any new training-time side channel. The honest reading: FlowA's broader-impact profile is \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{compute- reduction + rigor-of-reproducibility\}, not a new dual-use vector.

\subsection{Theory tightness analysis (Wave 185  ADDITIVE on 2.8.1)}
\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(a) Motivation: a distributional bound needs a distributional sanity check.\} Theorem 1 (2.8.1, lines 358460) bounds the bounded-Lipschitz (BL) distance between the framework's sampling distribution at \texttt{NFE} function evaluations and the framework's asymptotic target distribution. A reviewer who reads the bound literally may ask: \textbackslash\{\}textit\{is this bound tight on the empirical data?\} The bound's \texttt{B(NFE) = A\_g  exp(-NFE / B\_g) + C\_g  e\_} shape predicts a particular decay curve in NFE; if the empirical BL distance on the protein axis does not lie under this curve, the bound's claim scope needs to be re-located  the theorem does not become wrong, but it bounds a different quantity than a careless reader might infer. Wave 185 measures both sides of the comparison.

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(b) Empirical BL measurement via energy distance bootstrap.\} For each \texttt{(model, nfe)  \{lineageflow, kanzi\}  \{10, 50, 100, 150, 200, 300\}} (12 cells), Wave 185 P2 computes the 1-D pLDDT energy distance \texttt{d\_E(P\_framework\textasciicircum{}\{NFE\}, P\_baseline\textasciicircum{}\{NFE\})} (Szekely-Rizzo 2004, canonical BL-distance proxy on the protein axis) with percentile-bootstrap 95\% CIs (\texttt{n\_bootstrap=1000, seed=42},

\texttt{adaptive\_reflow.eval.coverage.energy\_distance\_with\_ci}). Empirical BL ranges from \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{0.0884\} (kanzi NFE=50) to \textbackslash\{\}textbf\{2.2326\} (lineageflow NFE=10)  see \texttt{verification\_outputs/wave185-p2-empirical-bl.csv} (12 rows  11 cols, full CI table).

\textbackslash\{\}textbf\{(c) Per-model tightness ratio at 6 NFE points.\} Wave 185 P3 combines (b) with the Theorem 1 RHS \texttt{B\_framework(NFE)} computed from \texttt{PaperQuantitiesSnapshot.for\_profile(g=sin(x), =0.1, c=1.0, =0.1)} (the framework regime defaults; profile \texttt{sin(x)} is the Wave 11 canonical reference profile). The tightness ratio

\texttt{ = empirical\_BL / B(NFE)} per cell is:
